facts card: 744 facts
cloudfront-504-origin-blocked-or-not-reachable
  claim: CloudFront returns an HTTP 504 status code if traffic to the origin is blocked by a firewall or security group, or if the origin is not accessible on the internet, or if the origin does not respond before the request expires.
  scope: CloudFront errors.
  link: [if traffic is blocked to the origin](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-504-origin-blocked-or-not-reachable)
  link: [the origin didn't respond before the request expired](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-504-origin-blocked-or-not-reachable)
cloudfront-alb-internal-needs-vpc-origins
  claim: Both internal and internet-facing Application Load Balancers can be used with CloudFront; an internal ALB inside private subnets works through VPC origins.
  scope: CloudFront with ALB.
  link: [internal Application Load Balancers inside private subnets](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-alb-internal-needs-vpc-origins)
  link: [both internal and internet-facing Application Load Balancers](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-alb-internal-needs-vpc-origins)
cloudfront-alb-restrict-custom-header-and-https
  claim: To allow access to an internet-facing ALB only through CloudFront, configure CloudFront to add a custom HTTP header, configure the ALB to forward only requests that contain that header, and require HTTPS.
  scope: CloudFront with internet-facing ALB.
  link: [add a custom HTTP header to requests](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-alb-restrict-custom-header-and-https)
  link: [only forward requests that contain the custom HTTP](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-alb-restrict-custom-header-and-https)
  link: [Require HTTPS to improve the security of this](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-alb-restrict-custom-header-and-https)
cloudfront-alias-record-apex-no-route53-query-charge
  claim: With a Route 53 alias resource record set for a CloudFront distribution you do not pay for Route 53 queries, and you can create an alias record for the root domain name (example.com), which DNS does not allow for CNAMEs.
  scope: CloudFront alternate domain name, Route 53.
  link: [you don’t pay for Route 53 queries](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-alias-record-apex-no-route53-query-charge)
  link: [root domain name (example.com)](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-alias-record-apex-no-route53-query-charge)
cloudfront-anycast-request-review-two-days
  claim: CloudFront support engineering reviews a request for an Anycast static IP list, and the review process might take up to two days.
  scope: per request; maximum; not adjustable.
  link: [up to two days](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-anycast-request-review-two-days)
cloudfront-anycast-requires-price-class-all
  claim: To use Anycast static IP lists with a CloudFront distribution, you must select Use all edge locations for the price class for the distribution.
  scope: per distribution.
  link: [Use all edge locations](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-anycast-requires-price-class-all)
  link: [you must select](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-anycast-requires-price-class-all)
cloudfront-anycast-static-ips-apex-domain
  claim: For routing an apex domain such as example.com directly to CloudFront, you request Anycast static IPs for that use case and add A records in your DNS that point the apex domain to CloudFront.
  scope: CloudFront Anycast static IPs.
  link: [routing of apex domains](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-anycast-static-ips-apex-domain)
  link: [add A records in your DNS](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-anycast-static-ips-apex-domain)
cloudfront-anycast-static-ips-purpose-account-dedicated
  claim: Anycast static IPs are dedicated to your AWS account and spread across geographic regions; you can allowlist them with network providers to waive data charges for viewers, or use them in outbound firewalls.
  scope: CloudFront Anycast static IPs.
  link: [dedicated to your AWS account](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-anycast-static-ips-purpose-account-dedicated)
  link: [waive data charges for viewers](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-anycast-static-ips-purpose-account-dedicated)
cloudfront-api-endpoints-https-only
  claim: The CloudFront API endpoints cloudfront.amazonaws.com and cloudfront-fips.amazonaws.com accept only HTTPS traffic.
  scope: CloudFront API.
  link: [only accept HTTPS traffic](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-api-endpoints-https-only)
  link: [cloudfront-fips.amazonaws.com](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-api-endpoints-https-only)
cloudfront-behavior-default-last
  claim: If a distribution has additional cache behaviors, the default cache behavior is always the last one processed; the others are processed in the order in which they are listed.
  scope: CloudFront cache behavior.
  link: [always the last to be processed](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-default-last)
  link: [processed in the order in which they're listed](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-default-last)
cloudfront-behavior-default-path-pattern-fixed
  claim: The path pattern of the default cache behavior is * (all files) and cannot be changed.
  scope: CloudFront default cache behavior.
  link: [default cache behavior is set to](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-default-path-pattern-fixed)
  link: [cannot be changed](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-default-path-pattern-fixed)
cloudfront-behavior-first-match-wins
  claim: CloudFront compares the requested path with the path patterns in the order in which the cache behaviors are listed, and the first match decides which cache behavior applies.
  scope: CloudFront cache behavior path pattern.
  link: [The first match determines which cache behavior](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-first-match-wins)
  link: [compared with path patterns in the order](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-first-match-wins)
cloudfront-behavior-one-origin-each
  claim: Each cache behavior names exactly one origin, so a distribution needs at least as many cache behaviors (the default one included) as it has origins to use all of them.
  scope: CloudFront cache behavior.
  link: [you specify the one origin](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-one-origin-each)
  link: [at least as many cache behaviors](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-one-origin-each)
cloudfront-behavior-path-pattern-ignores-query-cookies
  claim: CloudFront does not consider query strings or cookies when it evaluates a path pattern.
  scope: CloudFront cache behavior path pattern.
  link: [does not consider query strings or cookies](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-path-pattern-ignores-query-cookies)
  link: [when evaluating the path pattern](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-behavior-path-pattern-ignores-query-cookies)
cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-definition
  claim: Increasing the proportion of viewer requests served directly from the CloudFront cache instead of the origin is called improving the cache hit ratio.
  scope: CloudFront cache hit ratio.
  link: [the cache hit ratio](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-definition)
  link: [served directly from the CloudFront cache](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-definition)
cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-forward-only-needed-values
  claim: To raise the cache hit ratio, forward only the query string parameters for which the origin returns unique objects, use the same case and the same order for query string parameters, forward only specified cookies instead of all cookies, and create separate cache behaviors for static and dynamic content so that cookies are forwarded to the origin only for dynamic content.
  scope: CloudFront cache hit ratio.
  link: [forward only the query string parameters](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-forward-only-needed-values)
  link: [List parameters in the same order](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-forward-only-needed-values)
  link: [forward only specified cookies instead of forwarding all](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-forward-only-needed-values)
cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-longer-cache-duration
  claim: To increase the cache hit ratio, the origin can add a Cache-Control max-age directive to objects with the longest practical value; the shorter the cache duration, the more often CloudFront sends requests to the origin to check whether an object has changed and to get the latest version.
  scope: CloudFront cache hit ratio.
  link: [The shorter the cache duration](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-longer-cache-duration)
  link: [more frequently CloudFront sends requests to your origin](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-longer-cache-duration)
cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-origin-shield-layer
  claim: CloudFront Origin Shield can improve the cache hit ratio because it provides an additional layer of caching in front of the origin.
  scope: CloudFront Origin Shield.
  link: [an additional layer of caching in front](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-origin-shield-layer)
  link: [improve the cache hit ratio](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-hit-ratio-origin-shield-layer)
cloudfront-cache-key-values-forwarded-to-origin
  claim: All URL query strings, HTTP headers and cookies in the cache key are automatically included in origin requests; an origin request policy adds values to origin requests without adding them to the cache key.
  scope: CloudFront cache policy and origin request policy.
  link: [are automatically included in origin requests](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-key-values-forwarded-to-origin)
  link: [but *not* include in the cache key](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-key-values-forwarded-to-origin)
cloudfront-cache-policy-controls-cache-key
  claim: A cache policy controls the values (URL query strings, HTTP headers and cookies) that are included in the cache key, and so improves the cache hit ratio.
  scope: CloudFront cache policy.
  link: [controlling the values (URL query strings, HTTP headers,](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-policy-controls-cache-key)
  link: [included in the cache key](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-cache-policy-controls-cache-key)

  scope: CloudFront Field-Level Encryption; applies to PUT/POST requests before they are forwarded to the origin.

cloudfront-faq-flat-rate-monthly-renewal-no-annual-commitment
  claim: Flat-rate plans do not need an annual commitment to get the best available rates. Plans are purchased for the current month's billing period and automatically renew until canceled; when you cancel a plan, flat-rate pricing continues through the end of the current billing cycle, and the distribution and its plan resources then switch to pay-as-you-go pricing at the start of the next billing cycle.
  scope: CloudFront flat-rate plans.
  link: [do not need an annual commitment](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-flat-rate-monthly-renewal-no-annual-commitment)

  claim: For both new and existing distributions, you can enable or disable IPv6 per distribution in the CloudFront console or API.
  scope: per distribution, IPv6 on CloudFront.
  link: [enable / disable IPv6 per distribution](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-ipv6-enable-disable-per-distribution)
  link: [both new and existing distributions](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-ipv6-enable-disable-per-distribution)
cloudfront-faq-ipv6-trusted-signer-ip-whitelist-separate-distribution
  claim: To use IPv6 together with Trusted Signer URLs that have an IP whitelist, use two separate distributions: dedicate one distribution exclusively to the Trusted Signer URLs with IP whitelist and disable IPv6 for it, and use another distribution for all other content, which works with both IPv4 and IPv6.
  scope: CloudFront Trusted Signer URLs with IP whitelist and IPv6; you want IPv6 and Trusted Signer URLs with an IP whitelist.
  link: [use two separate distributions](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-ipv6-trusted-signer-ip-whitelist-separate-distribution)

cloudfront-faq-keyvaluestore-global-key-value-for-functions
  claim: CloudFront KeyValueStore is a global, low-latency, fully managed key-value data store. It lets CloudFront Functions retrieve key value data, so functions can be updated with new data independently of the function code.
  scope: CloudFront KeyValueStore with CloudFront Functions.
  link: [global, low-latency, fully managed key-value data store](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-keyvaluestore-global-key-value-for-functions)

cloudfront-faq-kinesis-shard-capacity
  claim: A single Kinesis shard can handle no more than 1 MB per second and 1,000 requests (log records) per second, which the CloudFront FAQ uses to size the stream for real-time logs.
  scope: per Kinesis shard; maximum; not adjustable.
  link: [1 MB per second](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-kinesis-shard-capacity)
  link: [1,000 requests (log records) per second](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-kinesis-shard-capacity)
cloudfront-faq-lambda-edge-for-heavy-computation
  claim: Use Lambda@Edge for computationally intensive operations: computations that take longer to complete (several milliseconds to seconds), depend on external third-party libraries, need integrations with other AWS services such as S3 or DynamoDB, or need network calls for data processing. CloudFront Functions was built for lightweight, high-scale, latency-sensitive request and response transformations.
  scope: choosing between CloudFront Functions and Lambda@Edge.
  link: [computationally intensive operations](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-lambda-edge-for-heavy-computation)
  link: [lightweight, high scale, and latency sensitive request/response transformations](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-lambda-edge-for-heavy-computation)
cloudfront-faq-lambda-edge-four-trigger-events
  claim: Lambda@Edge functions trigger automatically on four CloudFront events: viewer request, viewer response, origin request and origin response. Origin request runs when the edge does not have the requested object in its cache; origin response runs when the edge receives the origin's response.
  scope: Lambda@Edge triggers on CloudFront events.
  link: [Viewer Request](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-lambda-edge-four-trigger-events)
  link: [Origin Request](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-lambda-edge-four-trigger-events)
  link: [Origin Response](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-lambda-edge-four-trigger-events)
cloudfront-faq-multi-tenant-distribution-cannot-serve-traffic
  claim: A multi-tenant distribution defines the base configuration shared across domains, but unlike a standard distribution it cannot serve traffic directly. A Distribution Tenant represents a specific domain that inherits the base configuration.
  scope: CloudFront SaaS Manager, multi-tenant distributions.
  link: [cannot serve traffic directly](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-multi-tenant-distribution-cannot-serve-traffic)
  link: [base configuration that will be shared across domains](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-multi-tenant-distribution-cannot-serve-traffic)
cloudfront-faq-no-charge-aws-origin-transfer-out
  claim: When the origin is an AWS origin such as Amazon S3 or Amazon EC2, AWS no longer charges for data transfer out to CloudFront. This applies to data transfer from all AWS Regions to all global CloudFront edge locations.
  scope: data transfer from AWS origins to CloudFront edge locations; the origin is an AWS origin.

cloudfront-faq-origin-failover-backup-origin
  claim: For every origin in a CloudFront distribution you can assign a backup origin that automatically serves your traffic if the primary origin is unavailable. You choose which HTTP 4xx/5xx status codes returned from the primary origin trigger the failover, and the two origins can be any combination of AWS and non-AWS origins.
  scope: per origin of a CloudFront distribution.
  link: [assign a backup origin](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-failover-backup-origin)

cloudfront-faq-origin-mtls-origin-verifies-cloudfront
  claim: With origin mTLS, your application servers can verify that requests come only from your authorized CloudFront distributions before establishing secure connections. CloudFront presents a client certificate during the TLS handshake, and the origin server validates it against its trust store.
  scope: CloudFront origin mutual TLS authentication.

  claim: Origin mTLS replaces complex IP allowlists and custom header solutions with standardized certificate-based authentication, and suits origins hosted outside AWS or public origins on AWS.

cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-central-cache-layer
  claim: Origin Shield is a centralized caching layer that helps increase your cache hit ratio to reduce the load on your origin. When it is enabled, CloudFront routes all origin fetches through Origin Shield and only requests your origin if the content is not already in Origin Shield's cache.
  scope: CloudFront Origin Shield; Origin Shield is enabled.
  link: [centralized caching layer](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-central-cache-layer)
  link: [route all origin fetches through Origin Shield](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-central-cache-layer)
cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-collapses-requests-across-regions
  claim: Origin Shield decreases origin operating costs by collapsing requests across Regions so that as few as one request goes to your origin per object.
  scope: per object, Origin Shield; minimum.
  link: [collapsing requests across regions](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-collapses-requests-across-regions)
  link: [as few as one request](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-collapses-requests-across-regions)
cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-region-lowest-latency
  claim: When you enable Origin Shield, you should choose the AWS Region for Origin Shield that has the lowest latency to your origin. You can use Origin Shield with origins in an AWS Region and with origins that are not in AWS.
  scope: Origin Shield Region selection; Origin Shield is offered in AWS Regions where CloudFront has a regional edge cache.
  link: [lowest latency to your origin](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-region-lowest-latency)
  link: [origins that are not in AWS](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-region-lowest-latency)
cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-secondary-location-failover
  claim: Origin Shield Regions use a highly-available architecture that spans several Availability Zones. If the primary Origin Shield location is unavailable, CloudFront automatically routes a request to a secondary Origin Shield location.
  scope: CloudFront Origin Shield.
  link: [highly-available architecture that spans several Availability Zones](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-secondary-location-failover)
  link: [secondary Origin Shield location](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-secondary-location-failover)
cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-when-to-use
  claim: Origin Shield is ideal for workloads with viewers spread across different geographical regions, or workloads that involve just-in-time packaging for video streaming, on-the-fly image handling or similar processes. It can also reduce duplicate origin fetches across CDNs in a multi-CDN architecture.
  scope: CloudFront Origin Shield use cases.
  link: [viewers that are spread across different geographical regions](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-origin-shield-when-to-use)

cloudfront-faq-performance-edge-cache-persistent-connections
  claim: CloudFront caches copies of your content close to viewers in edge locations and regional edge caches. For files that are not cached there, it keeps persistent connections with your origin servers so the files are fetched as quickly as possible.
  scope: CloudFront delivery.

cloudfront-faq-post-put-delete-patch-not-cached
  claim: CloudFront does not cache the responses to POST, PUT, DELETE and PATCH requests; it proxies these requests back to the origin server. You may enable caching for the responses to OPTIONS requests.
  scope: CloudFront HTTP methods and caching; OPTIONS responses can be cached if you enable it.
  link: [POST, PUT, DELETE, and PATCH requests](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-post-put-delete-patch-not-cached)

cloudfront-faq-price-classes-exclude-expensive-locations
  claim: Price Classes let you reduce your delivery prices by excluding CloudFront's more expensive edge locations from your distribution. By default, CloudFront delivers content from its entire global network of edge locations to minimize end user latency.
  scope: CloudFront Price Classes, per distribution; default; you can choose another price class in the console or through the CloudFront API.
  link: [excluding Amazon CloudFront’s more expensive edge locations](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-price-classes-exclude-expensive-locations)
  link: [entire global network of edge locations](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-price-classes-exclude-expensive-locations)
cloudfront-faq-price-classes-higher-latency-and-billing
  claim: With a price class that does not include all locations, viewers outside the included locations may see higher latency. CloudFront may still occasionally serve requests from an edge location that is not in your price class, and then charges the rates of the least expensive location in your price class.
  scope: CloudFront Price Classes; a price class that does not include all locations.
  link: [may experience higher latency](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-price-classes-higher-latency-and-billing)

cloudfront-faq-sni-custom-ssl-modern-browsers-no-extra-cost
  claim: SNI Custom SSL relies on the SNI extension of Transport Layer Security, which lets several domains serve SSL traffic over the same IP address. Older browsers that do not support SNI cannot connect to load the HTTPS version of your content. SNI Custom SSL costs nothing beyond standard CloudFront data transfer and request fees.
  scope: SNI Custom SSL, CloudFront; viewers must support SNI.

  scope: CloudFront standard logs (access logs); charges depend on the log delivery destination.

cloudfront-faq-vpc-origins-no-secret-headers-free-private-ipv4
  claim: With VPC origins you can restrict access to your origins in a VPC to your CloudFront distributions, without complex configurations such as secret headers or Access Control Lists. You can also route to origins in a private subnet with internal IPv4 addresses, which are free of cost.
  scope: CloudFront VPC origins; origins in a private subnet.

cloudfront-faq-vpc-origins-versus-origin-mtls
  claim: Use VPC origins when your origins are AWS resources in private subnets of a VPC, such as Application Load Balancers or EC2 instances. Use origin mTLS when your origins are public AWS services like public ALBs or API Gateway endpoints, or are hosted outside AWS in on-premises data centers or with third-party providers.
  scope: CloudFront-to-origin connectivity; the choice depends on architecture, origin location and security requirements.
  link: [AWS resources deployed in private subnets](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-vpc-origins-versus-origin-mtls)
  link: [public AWS services like public ALBs](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-vpc-origins-versus-origin-mtls)
cloudfront-faq-waf-rules-allow-block-count
  claim: You can integrate a CloudFront distribution with AWS WAF and configure rules based on IP addresses, HTTP headers and custom URI strings. With these rules AWS WAF can block, allow or monitor (count) web requests.
  scope: AWS WAF integrated with a CloudFront distribution.
  link: [IP addresses, HTTP headers, and custom URI strings](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-waf-rules-allow-block-count)
  link: [block, allow, or monitor (count) web requests](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-waf-rules-allow-block-count)
cloudfront-faq-websocket-upgrade-header-101-and-wss
  claim: CloudFront establishes a WebSocket connection only when the client includes the 'Upgrade: websocket' header and the server responds with HTTP status code 101, confirming that it can switch to the WebSocket protocol. CloudFront supports encrypted WebSocket connections (WSS) using SSL/TLS.
  scope: CloudFront WebSocket connections; client sends the Upgrade: websocket header and the server answers with 101.
  link: [HTTP status code 101](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-websocket-upgrade-header-101-and-wss)
  link: [encrypted WebSocket connections (WSS)](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-websocket-upgrade-header-101-and-wss)
cloudfront-faq-zone-apex-anycast-static-ip
  claim: With CloudFront Anycast Static IPs you can point your apex domain to a CloudFront distribution using any DNS provider, by creating standard A records with the 3 static IP addresses that CloudFront provides.
  scope: apex domain, CloudFront Anycast Static IPs; Anycast Static IPs enabled for the apex domain use case.
  link: [any DNS provider](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-zone-apex-anycast-static-ip)
  link: [3 static IP addresses](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-zone-apex-anycast-static-ip)
cloudfront-faq-zone-apex-route53-alias
  claim: With Amazon Route 53 you can configure an ALIAS record that maps the apex (root) of your DNS name, such as example.com, to your CloudFront distribution. Route 53 does not charge for queries to ALIAS records that are mapped to a CloudFront distribution.
  scope: zone apex mapped to a CloudFront distribution with Route 53; the ALIAS record is mapped to a CloudFront distribution.
  link: [an ALIAS record](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-zone-apex-route53-alias)
  link: [doesn't charge for queries to ALIAS records](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-faq-zone-apex-route53-alias)
cloudfront-first-byte-forwarded-and-cached
  claim: CloudFront starts forwarding the object to the user as soon as the first byte arrives from the origin, and it also adds the object to the cache for the next request.
  scope: CloudFront request flow.
  link: [As soon as the first byte arrives](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-first-byte-forwarded-and-cached)
  link: [adds the object to the cache](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-first-byte-forwarded-and-cached)
cloudfront-fle-asymmetric-public-key
  claim: Field-level encryption uses asymmetric (public key) encryption: you give CloudFront a public key to encrypt the specified data, and only your private key can decrypt it.
  scope: CloudFront field-level encryption.
  link: [asymmetric encryption, also known as public key encryption](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-fle-asymmetric-public-key)
  link: [only your private key can do that](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-fle-asymmetric-public-key)
cloudfront-fle-encrypts-at-edge
  claim: Field-level encryption encrypts the sensitive information users upload at the edge, close to the user, and the data remains encrypted throughout the entire application stack.
  scope: CloudFront field-level encryption.
  link: [encrypted at the edge, close to the user](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-fle-encrypts-at-edge)
  link: [remains encrypted throughout your entire application stack](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-fle-encrypts-at-edge)
cloudfront-fle-layer-on-https-selected-fields
  claim: With CloudFront you can enforce secure end-to-end connections to origin servers by using HTTPS; field-level encryption adds an additional layer of security that protects specific data throughout system processing so that only certain applications can see it.
  scope: CloudFront field-level encryption.
  link: [adds an additional layer of security](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-fle-layer-on-https-selected-fields)
  link: [only certain applications can see it](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-fle-layer-on-https-selected-fields)

cloudfront-geo-restriction-country-level-whole-distribution
  claim: The CloudFront geographic restrictions feature restricts access to all of the files of a distribution and at the country level; geographic restrictions apply to an entire distribution. A third-party geolocation service is the option to restrict access to a subset of the files or at a finer granularity than the country level, and to apply different restrictions to different parts of your content you create separate CloudFront distributions or use a third-party geolocation service.
  scope: CloudFront geographic restrictions.
  link: [restrict access at the country level](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-geo-restriction-country-level-whole-distribution)

  claim: When geographic restrictions block a request, CloudFront returns HTTP status code 403 (Forbidden) to the user.

  link: [to the user](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-geo-restriction-returns-403)
cloudfront-geo-restriction-unknown-location-served
  claim: CloudFront determines user location with a third-party database, and if it cannot determine the location it serves the requested content.
  scope: CloudFront geographic restrictions.
  link: [CloudFront serves the content that the user has](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-geo-restriction-unknown-location-served)

cloudfront-grpc-bypasses-regional-edge-cache
  claim: CloudFront proxies gRPC requests directly to the origin and bypasses the regional edge cache, so gRPC does not support Lambda@Edge or Origin Shield.
  scope: CloudFront gRPC.
  link: [bypasses the Regional Edge Cache (REC)](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-grpc-bypasses-regional-edge-cache)
  link: [gRPC doesn't support Lambda@Edge or Origin Shield](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-grpc-bypasses-regional-edge-cache)
cloudfront-grpc-requires-http2-post-https
  claim: To handle gRPC, a distribution must include HTTP/2 among its supported HTTP versions and allow the POST method, and the gRPC origin must support HTTPS because only end-to-end HTTPS gRPC is supported.
  scope: CloudFront gRPC.
  link: [include HTTP/2 as one of the supported HTTP versions](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-grpc-requires-http2-post-https)
  link: [gRPC only supports end-to-end HTTPS](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-grpc-requires-http2-post-https)
cloudfront-grpc-supported-http2-proxied-no-cache
  claim: CloudFront supports gRPC, an open-source RPC framework built on HTTP/2, and proxies gRPC requests directly to the origins.
  scope: CloudFront gRPC.
  link: [gRPC, an open-source remote procedure call (RPC) framework built on HTTP/2](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-grpc-supported-http2-proxied-no-cache)
  link: [proxies them directly to your origins](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-grpc-supported-http2-proxied-no-cache)
cloudfront-http2-http3-viewer-requirements
  claim: HTTP/2 needs viewers that support TLSv1.2 or later and SNI; HTTP/3 needs viewers that support TLSv1.3 and SNI.
  scope: CloudFront supported HTTP versions.
  link: [HTTP/3, viewers must support TLSv1.3 and Server Name Indication (SNI)](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-http2-http3-viewer-requirements)
  link: [HTTP/2, viewers must support TLSv1.2 or later](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-http2-http3-viewer-requirements)

  claim: CloudFront supports HTTP/3 connection migration, which lets the viewer switch networks without losing the connection.

  link: [HTTP/3 connection migration](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-http3-connection-migration)
cloudfront-https-end-to-end-origin-always-https
  claim: To make sure that objects viewers get from CloudFront were encrypted when CloudFront fetched them from the origin, always use HTTPS between CloudFront and the origin.
  scope: CloudFront origin connection.
  link: [always use HTTPS between CloudFront and your origin](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-end-to-end-origin-always-https)

  claim: CloudFront does not support SSL renegotiation for viewer and origin requests, to help thwart renegotiation-type attacks.
  scope: CloudFront HTTPS.

  claim: With the HTTPS Only viewer protocol policy, CloudFront returns status code 403 (Forbidden) and no object when a viewer sends an HTTP request.
  scope: CloudFront viewer protocol policy.

  link: [does not return the object](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-only-returns-403)
cloudfront-https-origin-bad-certificate-502
  claim: If the origin returns an expired, invalid or self-signed certificate, or a certificate chain in the wrong order, CloudFront drops the TCP connection and returns status code 502 (Bad Gateway) to the viewer.
  scope: CloudFront custom origin HTTPS.
  link: [HTTP status code 502 (Bad Gateway)](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-origin-bad-certificate-502)
  link: [drops the TCP connection](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-origin-bad-certificate-502)
cloudfront-https-origin-domain-must-match
  claim: One of the domain names in the origin certificate must match the Origin Domain Name of the distribution; if none matches, CloudFront returns status code 502 (Bad Gateway) to the viewer.
  scope: CloudFront custom origin HTTPS.
  link: [must match the domain name that you specify](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-origin-domain-must-match)
  link: [`502 (Bad Gateway)` to the viewer](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-origin-domain-must-match)
cloudfront-https-origin-trusted-ca-no-self-signed
  claim: CloudFront verifies that the origin certificate was issued by a trusted certificate authority when it uses HTTPS to the origin, and a self-signed certificate cannot be used.
  scope: CloudFront custom origin HTTPS.
  link: [You can’t use a self-signed certificate](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-origin-trusted-ca-no-self-signed)
  link: [issued by a trusted certificate authority](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-origin-trusted-ca-no-self-signed)
cloudfront-https-redirect-301-get-head
  claim: With the Redirect HTTP to HTTPS policy, HTTP GET and HEAD requests are redirected to HTTPS and CloudFront returns status code 301 (Moved Permanently) with the new HTTPS URL.
  scope: CloudFront viewer protocol policy.
  link: [301 (Moved Permanently)](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-redirect-301-get-head)
  link: [automatically redirected to HTTPS requests](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-redirect-301-get-head)
cloudfront-https-redirect-307-other-methods
  claim: With the Redirect HTTP to HTTPS policy, a POST, PUT, DELETE, OPTIONS or PATCH request over HTTP (HTTP 1.1 or above) is redirected with status code 307 (Temporary Redirect), so the request is sent again with the same method and body.
  scope: CloudFront viewer protocol policy, HTTP 1.1 or above.
  link: [307 (Temporary Redirect)](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-redirect-307-other-methods)
  link: [using the same method and body payload](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-https-redirect-307-other-methods)

  claim: When an HTTP viewer request is redirected to HTTPS, CloudFront charges for both the HTTP request and the HTTPS request.

  claim: CloudFront shortens and removes round trips to establish connections, including for dynamic, uncacheable workloads like APIs: it terminates TLS close to viewers, maintains persistent connections to your origin, collapses requests to reduce load on origin servers, and carries traffic to your AWS application over AWS’ private global network rather than the public internet.

  claim: Regional edge caches sit between the origin server and the points of presence that serve content directly to viewers.

  claim: Dynamic requests, as determined at request time, do not go through regional edge caches and go directly to the origin.

  link: [do not flow through regional edge caches](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-dynamic-requests-skip)
cloudfront-rec-feature-parity-invalidation
  claim: Regional edge caches have feature parity with POPs, so an invalidation request removes an object from both POP caches and regional edge caches before it expires.
  scope: CloudFront invalidation, regional edge caches.
  link: [Regional edge caches have feature parity with POPs](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-feature-parity-invalidation)
  link: [removes an object from both POP caches](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-feature-parity-invalidation)
cloudfront-rec-larger-cache-than-pop
  claim: A regional edge cache has a larger cache than an individual POP, so objects stay in the cache longer at the nearest regional edge cache than at a POP.
  scope: CloudFront regional edge caches.
  link: [larger cache than an individual POP](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-larger-cache-than-pop)
  link: [objects remain in the cache longer](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-larger-cache-than-pop)
  link: [a larger cache](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-larger-cache-than-pop)
cloudfront-rec-proxy-methods-skip
  claim: Proxy HTTP methods (PUT, POST, PATCH, OPTIONS and DELETE) go directly from the POPs to the origin and do not go through the regional edge caches.
  scope: CloudFront regional edge caches.
  link: [go directly to the origin from the POPs](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-proxy-methods-skip)
  link: [do not proxy through the regional edge caches](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-proxy-methods-skip)
cloudfront-rec-shared-cache-persistent-connections
  claim: A regional edge cache is shared by all POPs in a region, which removes duplicate requests to the origin, and CloudFront keeps persistent connections with origin servers.
  scope: CloudFront regional edge caches.
  link: [POPs in a region share a local cache](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-shared-cache-persistent-connections)
  link: [persistent connections with origin servers](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-shared-cache-persistent-connections)
cloudfront-rec-skipped-same-region-s3
  claim: When the origin is an S3 bucket and the best regional edge cache is in the same AWS Region as the bucket, the POP skips the regional edge cache and goes directly to the bucket.
  scope: CloudFront regional edge caches, S3 origin.
  link: [in the same AWS Region as the S3 bucket](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-skipped-same-region-s3)
  link: [the POP skips the regional edge cache](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-rec-skipped-same-region-s3)
cloudfront-removed-origin-file-stays-until-expiry
  claim: If you remove files from the origin, CloudFront continues to show viewers content from the edge cache until the files expire.
  scope: CloudFront edge cache.
  link: [CloudFront will continue to show viewers content from](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-removed-origin-file-stays-until-expiry)
  link: [until the files expire](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-removed-origin-file-stays-until-expiry)
cloudfront-request-collapsing
  claim: If additional requests for the same object (with the same cache key) arrive at an edge location before CloudFront receives the response to the first request, CloudFront pauses before forwarding them to the origin, which reduces the load on the origin, and then sends the response from the original request to all the requests that it received while it was paused.
  scope: CloudFront origin requests.
  link: [This brief pause helps to reduce the load](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-request-collapsing)
  link: [sends the response from the original request](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-request-collapsing)
cloudfront-request-collapsing-disabled-by-caching-disabled
  claim: The managed cache policy CachingDisabled prevents request collapsing, and it also prevents caching.
  scope: CloudFront origin requests.
  link: [use the managed cache policy CachingDisabled](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-request-collapsing-disabled-by-caching-disabled)
  link: [which also prevents caching](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-request-collapsing-disabled-by-caching-disabled)
cloudfront-request-routed-lowest-latency-edge
  claim: When a user requests content served with CloudFront, the request is routed to the edge location that gives the lowest latency.
  scope: CloudFront viewer request.
  link: [edge location that provides the lowest latency](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-request-routed-lowest-latency-edge)
  link: [the request is routed to the edge location](../facts/cloudfront.md#cloudfront-request-routed-lowest-latency-edge)

cloudfront-signed-cookies-policy-canned-vs-custom
  claim: For CloudFront signed cookies, a canned policy lets you specify the date and time that users can no longer access your content, but not the date and time that users can begin to access it or the IP address range of the users; a custom policy lets you specify all three, the start time and the IP address range being optional.
  scope: CloudFront signed cookies.

  claim: Under the CloudFront SLA, a monthly uptime percentage less than 99.9% but greater than or equal to 99.0% earns a service credit of 10% of the total charges paid for CloudFront for the billing cycle in which the error occurred.

global-accelerator-failover-to-another-endpoint-group-ignores-dial
  claim: If an endpoint group has no healthy endpoint with a weight above zero, Global Accelerator tries to fail over to a healthy endpoint with a weight above zero in another endpoint group, and for this failover it ignores the traffic dial.

global-accelerator-fails-open-after-three-closest-groups
  claim: If no healthy endpoint with a weight above zero is found after trying the three closest endpoint groups, Global Accelerator routes traffic to a random endpoint in the endpoint group closest to the client (it fails open).
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator failover.

  claim: Custom routing accelerators do not provide external health checks for your VPC subnet endpoints or the EC2 instances in them. Your application monitors instance health and can fail over by directing user traffic to a different accelerator IP address and port combination.

If you add or remove endpoints after creating an accelerator, the mappings for existing endpoints do not change.

global-accelerator-faq-custom-routing-vs-standard-endpoints
  claim: Standard accelerators route traffic automatically to a healthy endpoint nearest to the user; you cannot deterministically route multiple users to a specific EC2 destination with them. Custom routing accelerators let you do that and support only VPC subnet endpoints, each containing one or more EC2 instances.

For workloads in a single Region used by clients in and around that Region, you can use an Application Load Balancer or Network Load Balancer; for a global client base, the FAQ recommends Global Accelerator.

Global Accelerator improves performance for a wide range of applications over TCP or UDP by proxying packets at the edge to applications running in one or more AWS Regions.

global-accelerator-faq-vs-elastic-ip-scope-and-advertisement
  claim: Global Accelerator IP addresses can be associated with endpoints in any number of AWS Regions and are advertised from AWS edge locations. Elastic IP addresses are tied to a single AWS resource in a single AWS Region and are advertised from a single AWS Region at a time.
  scope: Global Accelerator IP addresses compared with Elastic IP addresses.
  link: [in any number of AWS Regions](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-faq-vs-elastic-ip-scope-and-advertisement)

global-accelerator-recovery-existing-connections-not-moved
  claim: When Regions are healthy again Global Accelerator returns to regular routing, but established active connections are not moved; they stay on the zero weight Region until the connection is reset or the client opens a new one.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator failover.
  link: [established active connections are not moved](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-recovery-existing-connections-not-moved)
  link: [returns to regular routing behavior](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-recovery-existing-connections-not-moved)
global-accelerator-recovery-routing-about-30-seconds
  claim: When Regions are healthy again, routing typically starts back to healthy endpoints with traffic dials that are not set to zero in about 30 seconds. Established active connections are not moved: they continue to route to the zero-weight Region until the client or the server resets the connection, or until the client makes a new connection.
  scope: per accelerator, standard.
  link: [about 30 seconds](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-recovery-routing-about-30-seconds)
global-accelerator-resilience-continuous-health-monitoring
  claim: Global Accelerator continuously monitors endpoint health and, when an active endpoint is unhealthy, instantly starts directing traffic to another available endpoint, which supports a high-availability architecture.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator resilience.
  link: [continuously monitors the health of all endpoints](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-resilience-continuous-health-monitoring)
  link: [high-availability architecture](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-resilience-continuous-health-monitoring)
global-accelerator-same-account-endpoints-unless-cross-account
  claim: The resources added as endpoints and the accelerator must be owned by the same account unless cross-account support is configured; the targets behind a load balancer endpoint can be owned by different accounts.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator endpoints.
  link: [must be owned by the same account](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-same-account-endpoints-unless-cross-account)

global-accelerator-sla-multi-az-credit-10-percent
  claim: Under the Multi-AZ Global Accelerator SLA, a monthly uptime percentage below 99.99% but at or above 99.0% earns a service credit of 10%.
  scope: per Multi-AZ accelerator, per monthly billing cycle; fixed.
  link: [10%](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-sla-multi-az-credit-10-percent)
global-accelerator-sla-single-az-credit-10-percent

  scope: per Single-AZ accelerator, per monthly billing cycle; fixed.

global-accelerator-standard-endpoint-types

global-accelerator-standard-vs-custom-routing
  claim: A standard accelerator improves availability and directs traffic over the AWS global network to endpoints in the nearest Region to the client; a custom routing accelerator maps one or more users to a specific destination among many.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator accelerator types.
  link: [directs traffic over the AWS global network](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-standard-vs-custom-routing)
  link: [map one or more users to a specific](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-standard-vs-custom-routing)
global-accelerator-static-ips-anycast-from-edge
  claim: By default, Global Accelerator gives you static IP addresses for your accelerator, and these addresses are anycast from the AWS edge network.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator static IP addresses.
  link: [static IP addresses that you associate](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-static-ips-anycast-from-edge)
  link: [anycast from the AWS edge network](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-static-ips-anycast-from-edge)
global-accelerator-static-ips-kept-until-delete
  claim: The static IP addresses stay assigned to your accelerator as long as it exists, even if you disable it; when you delete the accelerator you lose the addresses.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator static IP addresses.
  link: [remain assigned to your accelerator for as long](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-static-ips-kept-until-delete)

global-accelerator-static-stability-health-checks-not-dials
  claim: A statically-stable Global Accelerator design uses the status of configured health checks to fail away from unhealthy endpoints; changing traffic dials, editing endpoint groups or removing endpoints during an impairment depends on the control plane.
  scope: Global Accelerator resilience.
  link: [use the status of the configured health checks](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-static-stability-health-checks-not-dials)
  link: [modify the AGA traffic dial percentages](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-static-stability-health-checks-not-dials)
global-accelerator-terminates-tcp-at-edge
  claim: Global Accelerator terminates TCP connections from clients at AWS edge locations and almost concurrently opens a new TCP connection to your endpoints, giving lower latency and higher throughput.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator performance.
  link: [terminates TCP connections from clients at AWS edge](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-terminates-tcp-at-edge)
  link: [faster response times (lower latency) and increased throughput](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-terminates-tcp-at-edge)
global-accelerator-traffic-dial-blue-green-regional-maintenance
  claim: The traffic dial lets you do performance testing or blue/green deployment testing for new releases across different AWS Regions; to upgrade or maintain a Region you first dial its traffic down to zero.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator traffic dial.
  link: [performance testing or blue/green deployment testing](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-traffic-dial-blue-green-regional-maintenance)

global-accelerator-traffic-dial-new-connections-only
  claim: A change to a traffic dial applies only to new connections: existing connections are not terminated, and health checks are not affected by traffic dial settings.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator traffic dial.

global-accelerator-traffic-dial-percent-of-directed-traffic
  claim: The traffic dial of an endpoint group controls the percentage of traffic directed to that group, applied only to traffic already directed to it and not to all listener traffic.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator traffic dial.
  link: [control the percentage of traffic that is directed](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-traffic-dial-percent-of-directed-traffic)

global-accelerator-traffic-on-aws-network-congestion-free
  claim: Traffic travels over the well-monitored, congestion-free, redundant AWS global network to the endpoint, and Global Accelerator maximizes the time traffic stays on the AWS network.

global-accelerator-udp-fragments-forwarded-tcp-dropped
  claim: Global Accelerator forwards UDP fragments to the configured endpoint, which reassembles the packet, and drops TCP fragments at the edge because the AWS network does not support them.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator.
  link: [forwards UDP fragments to the configured endpoint](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-udp-fragments-forwarded-tcp-dropped)
  link: [drops TCP fragments at the edge](../facts/global-accelerator.md#global-accelerator-udp-fragments-forwarded-tcp-dropped)
global-accelerator-udp-listener-tcp-health-check
  claim: For EC2 instance or Elastic IP address endpoints behind a UDP listener, Global Accelerator health-checks with the TCP protocol on the listener port, so the endpoint must run a TCP server.
  scope: AWS Global Accelerator health checks.

global-accelerator-use-case-disaster-recovery-reroute
